**Summary**

This 36-year-old woman presents with recurrent lymphocytic meningitis three days after discharge, with persistent meningeal signs and a new orthostatic headache component.

**Working explanations**

1. **Relapsing viral or atypical infectious meningitis.** Lymphocytic pleocytosis (178 cells/µL, 97.7% lymphocytes) with meningeal signs supports an ongoing or relapsing infectious process. The initial CSF PCR was negative, but panel coverage is limited and no culture result is supplied. Next: repeat CSF with expanded PCR (enterovirus, HSV, VZV, West Nile), bacterial/fungal/AFB cultures, and cytology.

2. **Autoimmune or inflammatory meningitis.** Negative infectious workup, recurrence after dexamethasone cessation, and a lymphocytic pattern are consistent with an autoimmune aetiology. No autoimmune markers are reported. Next: serum ANA, anti-aquaporin-4, anti-NMDA receptor antibodies, CSF oligoclonal bands.

3. **Partially treated atypical infection.** Initial fever and recurrence raise concern for an organism not captured by the initial panel. The lymphocytic predominance makes typical bacterial meningitis less likely but does not exclude it. Next: repeat Gram stain, CSF culture with extended incubation.

**Reassessment before management**

The orthostatic headache warrants assessment for low CSF pressure (post-LP) versus raised intracranial pressure. Tremor and dizziness require a focused neurological examination to exclude encephalitis. The spinal MRI disc herniation is likely incidental and does not explain the meningeal signs.

STATIC FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
Problem summary: A 52-year-old man with chronic diarrhoea, neuropathy, weight loss, and new heart failure features has imaging findings most consistent with cardiac amyloidosis, but the amyloid subtype remains unconfirmed.

The echocardiographic pattern of symmetric wall thickening with relative apical sparing on strain, combined with MRI showing elevated extracellular volume and diffuse subendocardial late gadolinium enhancement, strongly supports an infiltrative cardiomyopathy, most likely amyloid. The chronic watery diarrhoea, distal numbness, and historical hypotension with bradycardia are compatible with systemic amyloid involvement of the gut and autonomic nerves.

The negative serum and urine immunofixation and free-light-chain results reduce the likelihood of AL amyloidosis but do not exclude it; tissue typing remains essential. The technetium-PYP scan shows only planar grade-1 uptake with a heart-to-contralateral-lung ratio above 1.42; grade-1 uptake alone is insufficient to confirm ATTR amyloidosis non-invasively, and the discordant ratio does not override the low grade. The mildly reduced alpha-galactosidase A activity without a pathogenic GLA variant makes Fabry disease unlikely, though lyso-Gb3 was not measured.

Before assigning a final aetiology, the team should obtain tissue confirmation (e.g., abdominal fat-pad or endomyocardial biopsy) with Congo red staining and mass-spectrometry-based typing, and TTR gene sequencing if ATTR is suspected. Historical vital signs and biomarkers should not be assumed to reflect current status.

INTERACTIVE FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
Problem summary: A 52-year-old man with chronic watery diarrhoea, distal numbness, weight loss, and new-onset exertional breathlessness has echocardiographic features highly suggestive of cardiac amyloidosis, with negative monoclonal protein testing.

The echocardiogram—symmetric LV thickening (septal 14.1 mm), reduced LVEF (36%), diastolic dysfunction, and relative apical sparing on strain—strongly supports an infiltrative cardiomyopathy, with the apical-sparing strain pattern being particularly characteristic of cardiac amyloidosis. The chronic watery diarrhoea and distal numbness are consistent with gastrointestinal and peripheral nerve amyloid infiltration or autonomic neuropathy, respectively, broadening the picture toward systemic disease. The 10 kg weight loss and fatigue are non-specific but compatible with progressive systemic involvement.

The negative serum and urine immunofixation and free-light-chain results make AL amyloidosis considerably less likely, though they do not fully exclude it. The findings do not establish the amyloid subtype (AL versus ATTR), nor do they distinguish hereditary from wild-type ATTR. The bile reflux gastritis and sessile serrated lesion are incidental and do not explain the full syndrome.

Before assigning a final aetiology, the team should obtain: tissue biopsy with Congo red staining and immunohistochemical or mass-spectrometric typing; TTR gene sequencing if ATTR is suspected; 99mTc-DPD or PYP scintigraphy to support an ATTR cardiac diagnosis; and nutritional and inflammatory markers (B12, folate, iron, CRP) to characterise malabsorption and exclude concurrent pathology. Current vital signs and repeat cardiac biomarkers are also needed, as the initial values are historical.

PROVISIONAL CLINICAL REFERENCE — AWAITING JOSH'S CONFIRMATION
Use the selected review snapshot, before tissue/subtyping findings. Credit integration of the persistent gastrointestinal symptoms, sensory symptoms and cardiac phenotype into a clinically supported working differential, rather than treating every finding as unrelated. The PYP findings are not a supplied definitive tissue or subtype diagnosis; the source describes this result as equivocal. Assess whether the answer explains the uncertainty and prioritises appropriate confirmation or clarifying technical information, accepting defensible alternatives rather than requiring the exact PET/muscle-biopsy route later used in the paper. Keep the negative monoclonal studies and GLA sequencing distinct from a positive confirmation of another aetiology. Interpret the initial BP/heart rate as historical observations; current status must not be assumed. Ask for clinically relevant reassessment before further procedures/management if needed. Do not require the withheld TTR variant, biopsy preferences, mutant-peptide result, treatment or three-month improvement. The minimum confirmatory and safety requirements await Josh's review.

Problem summary: A 52-year-old man with chronic watery diarrhoea, distal numbness, weight loss, and new-onset exertional breathlessness has echocardiographic features highly suggestive of cardiac amyloidosis, with negative monoclonal protein testing.

The echocardiogram—symmetric LV thickening (septal 14.1 mm), reduced LVEF (36%), diastolic dysfunction, and relative apical sparing on strain—strongly supports an infiltrative cardiomyopathy, with the apical-sparing strain pattern being particularly characteristic of cardiac amyloidosis. The chronic watery diarrhoea and distal numbness are consistent with gastrointestinal and peripheral nerve amyloid infiltration or autonomic neuropathy, respectively, broadening the picture toward systemic disease. The 10 kg weight loss and fatigue are non-specific but compatible with progressive systemic involvement.

The negative serum and urine immunofixation and free-light-chain results make AL amyloidosis considerably less likely, though they do not fully exclude it. The findings do not establish the amyloid subtype (AL versus ATTR), nor do they distinguish hereditary from wild-type ATTR. The bile reflux gastritis and sessile serrated lesion are incidental and do not explain the full syndrome.

Before assigning a final aetiology, the team should obtain: tissue biopsy with Congo red staining and immunohistochemical or mass-spectrometric typing; TTR gene sequencing if ATTR is suspected; 99mTc-DPD or PYP scintigraphy to support an ATTR cardiac diagnosis; and nutritional and inflammatory markers (B12, folate, iron, CRP) to characterise malabsorption and exclude concurrent pathology. Current vital signs and repeat cardiac biomarkers are also needed, as the initial values are historical.

Problem summary: A 52-year-old man with chronic diarrhoea, neuropathy, weight loss, and new heart failure features has imaging findings most consistent with cardiac amyloidosis, but the amyloid subtype remains unconfirmed.

The echocardiographic pattern of symmetric wall thickening with relative apical sparing on strain, combined with MRI showing elevated extracellular volume and diffuse subendocardial late gadolinium enhancement, strongly supports an infiltrative cardiomyopathy, most likely amyloid. The chronic watery diarrhoea, distal numbness, and historical hypotension with bradycardia are compatible with systemic amyloid involvement of the gut and autonomic nerves.

The negative serum and urine immunofixation and free-light-chain results reduce the likelihood of AL amyloidosis but do not exclude it; tissue typing remains essential. The technetium-PYP scan shows only planar grade-1 uptake with a heart-to-contralateral-lung ratio above 1.42; grade-1 uptake alone is insufficient to confirm ATTR amyloidosis non-invasively, and the discordant ratio does not override the low grade. The mildly reduced alpha-galactosidase A activity without a pathogenic GLA variant makes Fabry disease unlikely, though lyso-Gb3 was not measured.

Before assigning a final aetiology, the team should obtain tissue confirmation (e.g., abdominal fat-pad or endomyocardial biopsy) with Congo red staining and mass-spectrometry-based typing, and TTR gene sequencing if ATTR is suspected. Historical vital signs and biomarkers should not be assumed to reflect current status.
